8  Radar Collection Parameters 
This section summarizes optional radar specific parameters that may be provided with a 
given product.  These parameters allow the producer of the CPHD product to describe the 
transmit waveform, the manner in which it was demodulated and sampled, and the antenna(s) 
used during transmission and reception.  
8.1 Transmit & Receive Parameters 
CPHD provides support for describing the details of the collection’s transmit waveform(s) 
and receive window(s).  There are many collection paradigms that could result in multiple 
transmit waveforms and their corresponding receive windows.  Most prominent among these 
are orthogonal phase codes and step chirp pulse sequences. 
The basics of a transmit waveform are captured by the four required parameters—pulse 
length (PulseLength), bandwidth (RFBandwidth), center frequency (FreqCenter), and 
polarization (Polarization).  The optional chirp rate parameter (LFMRate) should be included 
if the waveform is a linear frequency modulation chirp (LFMC).  The chirp rate may be 
omitted or set equal to zero for a non-LFM waveform.  The relationship of the metadata 
parameters to the transmit waveform is depicted in Figure 8-1. 
 
 
 
Figure 8-1  Transmit Waveform and Receive Window for LFM 
Diagram showing the time vs power and frequency for 4 targets. 

CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
116 
The receive window is described by five required parameters—window length 
(WindowLength), sample rate (SampleRate), filter bandwidth (IFFilterBW), center frequency 
(FreqCenter), and polarization (Polarization).  Figure 8-1 shows how two of these parameters 
along with the optional demodulation chirp rate (LFMRate) describe the demodulation signal 
for an LFMC.  The LFMRate may be omitted or set equal to zero for a constant frequency 
demodulation. 
The impact of the other two parameters—sample rate and filter bandwidth—is shown in 
Figure 8-2 below.  Targets B, C, & D are fully captured by the receive window and post-
demodulation passband.  Target A is closer to the sensor and arrives too early to be fully 
captured by the receive window.  Additionally, while the sample rate is high enough to 
capture Target A’s signal unaliased, its post-demodulation frequency is outside the anti-
aliasing filter’s passband.  Its echo is at attenuated and may be phase distorted. 
 
 
 
 
Figure 8-2  Receive Window Details for LFM Chirp 
Diagram showing time vs frequency pre and post demodulation. 
The transmit waveform and receive descriptions each contain an optional parameter that 
deals with signal power.  The peak transmit power (TxWF / Power) is used to describe the 
peak transmit power at the antenna interface.  The receiver path gain (Rcv / PathGain) is used 
to describe the total gain the signal will experience after the antenna interface.  The PathGain 
includes both the analog signal gain from the antenna reference point to the A/D converters 
and the processing gain applied in in compensation processing. 
This power/gain segmentation of the radar payload is depicted for a monostatic collect with a 
single antenna in Figure 8-3.  Note that the antenna interface is the same for the transmit and 
receive signal paths.  This means that the output power intensity along the boresight is a 
combination of the peak transmit power and absolute antenna boresight gain (GainZero).  
The boresight GainZero is a combination of the aperture efficiency and the boresight 
directivity.  The total gain on receive is a combination of the receiver path gain and the 
absolute antenna gain.  Given the optional parameters of transmit power, receiver path gain, 
absolute gain for the antenna(s), and the involved geometry, power intensity at the scene and 
of the observed echoes can be derived.  For a bistatic collection, the transmit and receive 
antennas are separate and, in general, will have unique boresight gain values.  Figure 8-4 
shows an example bistatic system. 

CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
117 
 
 
 
Figure 8-3  Monostatic Transmitter, Receiver, and Antenna Subsystems 
Diagram showing a notional block diagram for a monostatic radar system. 
 
 
 
 
Figure 8-4  Bistatic Transmitter, Receiver, and Antenna Subsystems 
Diagram showing a notional block diagram for a bistatic radar system. 

CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
118 
8.2 Antenna Parameters 
The CPHD metadata structure includes parameters that describe the antenna(s) that were 
used during the SAR imaging collection.  The provided parameters describe the antenna 
orientation and mainlobe boresight pointing as a function of time.  The parameters also 
describe the antenna beam shape gain and phase patterns that may be constant or may vary 
with time and frequency. 
The antenna beam shape and pointing information is important for use in forming fully 
exploitable SAR image products with the best quality.  The antenna metadata is optional in a 
CPHD product but is encouraged to be included in all products.  For all SAR sensors, the 
properties of the antennas are critical in predicting performance and in properly tasking the 
imaging collections.  This information should be provided with the collected phase history 
data for use in forming the image products.  It is incumbent on the producers to provide the 
best available metadata to enable users of the data to make the best quality and best 
characterized products possible. 
The CPHD antenna metadata structure specifies the pointing and one-way beam shape 
parameters for the antennas used during a collection.  The metadata structure uses the 
following constructs to specify the antenna pointing and beam shape during a collection. 
(1) Antenna Coordinate Frame (ACF):  An ACF is used to specify an antenna 
physical structure and its orientation vs. time.  The ACF is a right-handed 
coordinate frame with coordinate (ACX, ACY, ACZ).  The +ACZ axis points 
to the “front” of the antenna. One or more ACFs may be specified in a 
product. 
(2) Antenna Phase Center (APC):  An APC is the single point located on the 
antenna structure that is the effective point source for transmitted pulses 
and/or the effective point of reception for received signals.  Each APC is 
specified as fixed position in the ACF of one antenna. 
(3) Antenna Pattern (APAT):  An antenna pattern specifies the one-way beam 
shape gain pattern and phase pattern for a transmit and/or receive aperture 
used during the collection. Each antenna pattern structure specifies an array 
pattern and an element pattern.  A product may include one or more antenna 
patterns. 
(4) Electrical Boresight (EB) Steering Vector:  The EB steering vector specifies 
the mainlobe pointing direction of the antenna beam shape pattern.  The EB 
steering vector vs. time is specified by a unit vector in ACF coordinates.  The 
EB SV is specified using two direction cosines (EB_DCX and EB_DCY).  
For a mechanically steered antenna, the EB steering vector is aligned with 
the +ACZ axis (EB_DCX = EB_DCY = 0). 
For each data channel contained in a CPHD product, the PVP array includes the transmit 
APC position and the receive APC position for each signal vector.  When antenna metadata 
is provided, the transmit antenna and the receive antenna used to collect the PHD for the 
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
119 
channel are specified.  For each antenna, the APC, ACF, the APAT and the EB SV are 
specified vs. time.  For a monostatic collection, the same parameter set (i.e., same APC, 
ACF, APAT, and EB) may be specified on transmit and receive.  For bistatic collections, a 
unique parameter set is specified for the antenna located on each sensor. 
8.2.1 Antenna Coordinate Frames 
For each physical antenna used during a collection, an ACF is defined that is fixed relative to 
the structure of the antenna.  For most antennas, the ACF is logically assigned or aligned to 
the mechanical frame that was used to build and characterize the antenna.  For ACF 
metadata, the ACX and ACY axes are in the plane of the antenna and the +ACZ points to the 
“front” of the antenna and is referred to as the mechanical boresight.  Shown in Figure 8-5 is 
an example parabolic reflector antenna and the ACF axes.  Shown in Figure 8-6 is an 
example electronically steered antenna (ESA) and the ACF axes.  
Each ACF included in a product is assigned a unique ACF identifier (ACF_ID).  For most 
collections, a single antenna is used and one ACF is included in a product.  The following 
guidance is provided for populating the ACF metadata parameters. 
(1) Each ACF orientation vs. time is provided using uACX(t) and uACY(t) 
vector polynomials.  See Table 11-10.  For orbiting sensors, the unit vector 
polynomials typically provide an accurate format for the best available 
orientation vs. time data. 
(2) The uACX and uACY unit vectors may also be provided via the Per Vector 
Parameters array.  See Table 2-2 and Table 11-6.  The ACF PVPs are optional 
but encouraged to be included in all products.  For many airborne collections, 
the high sample rate of the PVPs provide the most accurate format for 
capturing the ACF variation with time. 
(3) For products that include the ACF PVPs, the parameter UseACFPVP = TRUE 
indicates that the PVPs provide a more accurate reporting of the ACF 
orientation and should be used for further processing.  The method and 
conditions for setting UseACFPVP is a product design choice. 
For all sensors and data processing systems, providing the ACF PVPs is highly encouraged.  
It is expected that a future version of CPHD will only allow the PVP-based parameters and 
the vector polynomials will be eliminated.   
Guidance on how to compute the ACF unit vectors, uACX(t) and uACY(t) is provided in the 
SICD Design & Implementation Description Document, NGA.STND.0024-1. 
 
 
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
120 
8.2.2 Antenna Phase Centers 
A product may contain one or more APCs.  Each APC is assigned a unique APC_ID.  The 
APC construct in the antenna metadata is populated per the following conventions. 
(1) Each APC is assigned to a single ACF and specified at a fixed position in the 
ACF frame. More than one APC may be assigned to a single ACF. 
(2) The APC positions within ACF allow a straight forward method for 
specifying the baseline between two APCs that are displaced within an ACF.  
The APC displacement may useful in the subsequent processing of the 
CPHD signal arrays.  Shown in Figure 8-6 is an example with displaced 
APCs on receive. 
(2) For each data channel, an APC is assigned as the transmit APC (Tx_APC) 
and an APC is specified as the receive APC (Rcv_APC).  For a monostatic 
collection, the same APC may be assigned to both the Tx_APC and the 
Rcv_APC.   
(3) For each data channel, the assigned APCs indicate which ACFs are used on 
transmit and on receive.  For example, the transmit APC indicates which 
ACF metadata is used to compute the transmit antenna orientation.  The 
receive APC indicates which ACF metadata is used to compute the receive 
antenna orientation. 
(4) For a product that includes the ACF PVPs, the transmit and receive ACF unit 
vectors may be taken from the PVPs without concern for which APCs are 
assigned to the data channel.     
 
 
 
 
Figure 8-5  Parabolic Reflector Antenna  
Example reflector antenna with a single APC located behind the primary reflector.

8.2.3 Antenna Patterns 
A CPHD product may contain one or more Antenna Pattern (APAT) structures.  Each APAT 
is assigned a unique APAT_ID.  Each APAT structure provides parameters that specify a 
one-way antenna pattern that was used to collect the signal array for one or more of the data 
channels.  For each data channel, a APAT is assigned as the transmit antenna pattern 
(TxAPAT) and an APAT is assigned as the receive antenna pattern (RcvAPAT).  The same 
APAT may be assigned to both the TxAPAT and the RcvAPAT.    
The APAT structure includes components that specify the beam shape gain and phase vs. 
pointing direction as well as optional parameters that specify how the patterns vary with 
frequency.  See Table 11-10.  For each APAT, two types of gain and phase patterns are 
provided: an array pattern and an element pattern.  Together these are used to form the beam 
shape pattern that has the following attributes.  The element pattern is fundamental to an 
electronically scanned array (ESA).  It is provided in all APATs and may be set equal to 0 for 
a mechanically scanned antenna. 
(1) The array pattern is provided for reference frequency f_0.  The array pattern 
provides the mainlobe gain that is a function of the size of the 
antenna/aperture.  For an ESA, it is the array pattern that is electrically 
steered. 
(2) The element pattern is also provided for reference f_0.  The element pattern 
provides the gain pattern for the transmit/receive (T/R) elements for ESA.  
The element pattern for a mechanically steered antenna is set to 0 dB gain and 
0 phase.

CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
122 
(3) The beam shape pattern is defined to be the antenna gain and phase in a given 
direction relative to the gain and phase on mechanical boresight at frequency 
f_0.  For an ESA, the EB is steered to be in +ACZ direction (EB_DCX = 
EB_DCY = 0). 
The array and element patterns may be provided in a given product in two ways.  For all 
products, the gain and phase patterns are provided as two-dimensional polynomial functions 
of the pointing direction specified.  The pointing direction is specified in the ACF using 
direction cosines DCX and DCY.  The array pattern polynomial specifies the shape for the 
portion of the mainlobe used in most imaging collections.  For each pattern, a sampled 
pattern may be provided via an Antenna Gain/Phase support array.  The sample pattern can 
be used to provide a more complete pattern that may include the best available estimate for 
the mainlobe and sidelobes. 
For each antenna pattern, an optional set of parameters may be provided and used to compute 
the polarization orientation on transmit and receive vs. time.  The parameters provided are 
the E-field relative amplitudes and phases.    The parameters are AmpX, AmpY, and PhaseY.  
The amplitudes and phases may be combined with ACF unit vectors uACX(t) and uACY(t) 
to compute the polarization orientation vs. time.  For example, the transmit E-field on 
mechanical boresight for a signal vector v and transmit time txc is computed as follows. 
 E_X(v, t) =  uACX(txc(v))AmpXcos{2f_0(t – txc(v))} +  
    uACY(txc(v))AmpYcos{2(f_0(t – txc(v)) + PhaseY)} 
For the E-field orientation in other pointing directions is computed by projecting the ACX 
and ACY components normal to the pointing direction.  For point directions near the +ACZ 
axis, the ACX and ACY components projected normal to the line-of-sight remain nearly 
orthogonal. 
8.2.4 Electrical Boresight Steering Vectors 
The construct of an Electrical Boresight (EB) steering vector (SV) is fundamental to 
describing the mainlobe pointing and beam shape pattern for ESAs.  The construct is 
included for all antenna pattern metadata in a CPHD product.  The EB SV metadata provided 
in CPHD products has the following attributes. 
(1) The EB SV is specified as a unit vector in ACF coordinates that points from 
the APC toward the imaged scene. 
(2) The EB SV is a specified using two direction cosines (EB_DCX and 
EB_DCY).  Refer to Figure 8-7.  The EB_DCZ may be computed as shown in 
the figure and is always positive. 
(3) For a mechanically steered antenna, the EB steering vector components are 
provided and are to (0,0).  The EB is aligned with the +ACZ axis. 
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
123 
(4) The EB SV is provided for frequency f = f_0 where the f_0 is the value 
specified in the APAT structure.  For an ESA that has the EB shift as a 
function of frequency, optional parameters in the APAT structure are 
provided that are used to compute the shift vs. frequency. 
The EB SV may be provided in a given product in two ways.  For each APAT, the EB SV is 
provided using polynomial functions of time for the EB_DCX and EB_DCY components 
(XML:  /CPHD/Antenna/AntPattern/EB).  The EB SV data may also be provided in the PVP 
array for each signal vector.  For an ESA that can be steered at a rapid rate, the EB PVPs will 
provide the most accurate format for capturing the EB SV variation with time. 
Shown in Figure 8-7 is an example EB SV at an instant in time.  Shown in the left side of the 
figure is the EB in the ACF coordinate frame.  For the example shown, the APC is located at 
the origin of the ACF.  All EB SVs have EB_DCZ > 0 and point to the “front” half of the 
antenna.  Shown in the right half of the figure is a two-dimensional representation of the EB 
SV.  Only points that lie inside the unit circle in the (DCX, DCY) plane represent valid 
pointing directions.

9  Error Parameters 
For any CPHD product, an optional set of metadata parameters may be included that are the 
error statistics for parameters that describe the collection and/or are used in the compensation 
processing to form the product.  The set of parameters are included in the CPHD / 
ErrorParameters branch of the XML instance.  See Table 11-12.  The optional parameters are 
the expected statistics for the primary sources of error that lead to geolocation errors in a 
SAR image that is formed from the CPHD product.  The error parameters support both 
monostatic and bistatic SAR imaging collections. 
For a monostatic collection, the ErrorParameters / Monostatic branch is included in the XML 
instance.  The Monostatic parameters include the expected statistics for errors in the 
estimated platform position and velocity, errors in the radar sensor timing, and errors in the 
estimated propagation delays.  Monostatic SAR imaging systems are routinely designed such 
that the output SAR map products have precisely located pixel grids.  The error statistics 
included in the CPHD product may be used to compute the expected statistics for the errors 
in geolocation estimates derived from the map products formed from the signal arrays.  
For a bistatic collection, the ErrorParameters / Bistatic branch is included in the XML 
instance.  The bistatic parameters include the expected error statistics for both the transmit 
platform and the receive platform.  For each platform, the parameters include the statistics for 
errors in the estimated platform position and velocity and errors in the radar sensor timing.  A 
bistatic SAR imaging system will include one or more methods to achieve accurate time 
synchronization between transmit and receive platforms.  For a bistatic CPHD product, the 
error statistics include the expected accuracy of the time synchronization that was achieved 
when the compensation processing was performed.  For a bistatic system, errors in time 
synchronization are typically the dominate source of geolocation error in the bistatic SAR 
image products. 
 
9.1 Monostatic Error Parameters 
For a monostatic CPHD (Collect Type = MONOSTATIC), the expected statistics are 
provided for the primary sources of geolocation error in the SAR map products.  The 
statistics are provided for three types of errors. 
Position & Velocity Errors 
The elements of the position and velocity covariance matrix are included.  The variance 
parameters (i.e., the terms along the main diagonal) are always specified.  The correlation 
coefficients may also be included.  The decorrelation versus time for position estimates may 
also be specified with a simple linear model. 
Radar Sensor Errors 
The primary error sources associated with the radar sensor are a range bias and an error in the 
radar timing clock frequency.  The error in estimated collection start time may be included 
but is not required. 
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
125 
Propagation Model Errors 
The error statistics associated with the predicted propagation delays due to the troposphere 
and the ionosphere may be included.  Error decorrelation parameters may also be included. 
 
9.2 Bistatic Error Parameters 
For a bistatic CPHD (Collect Type = BISTATIC), the expected statistics are provided for the 
transmit platform and the receive platform.  The statistics are provided for two types of 
errors. 
Position & Velocity Errors 
The elements of the position and velocity covariance matrix are included.  The variance 
parameters (i.e., the terms along the main diagonal) are always specified.  The correlation 
coefficients may also be included.  The decorrelation versus time for position estimates may 
also be specified with a simple linear model. 
Radar Sensor Errors 
For bistatic imaging, the error in geolocation is often dominated by the time synchronization 
error between transmit and receive platforms.  For each platform, the expected statistics for 
the error in estimated collection start time is provided.  Also provided are the expected 
statistics for the error in the radar payload clock frequency that leads to errors in the 
estimated transmit frequency and/or the estimated receive demodulation frequency.  
 
  
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
126 
10  CPHD XML Description 
The CPHD XML structure is expressed using the eXtensible Markup Language (XML), 
Version 1.0.  The XML structure is composed of required parameters, optional parameters 
and choice parameters.  The defined set of parameters allows for a full description of the 
CPHD signal arrays and how they were collected and processed.  Also provided is a method 
for including “added” or program specific parameters.  The program specific parameters are 
defined per a program specific Product Design Document.  See Section 3. 
10.1 XML Parameter Types 
The XML structure is composed of the parameter types listed in Table 10-1.  These 
parameter types are formally defined in the CPHD XML schema that is also part of this 
standard.  The parameter types listed in Table 10-1 are for convenience.  
 For reference:  http://www.w3.org/TR/2004/REC-xmlschema-2-20041028 
 
Table 10-1  XML Parameter Types  
Type Description Guidance 
TXT Value is a string of text characters.  See XML 
datatype xs:string. 
 
ENU 
Value can be a string of characters or an integer. 
For each ENU, a set of allowed values is specified.  
Integer values are specified in decimal format.  
The list of allowed values specified 
uniquely for each parameter. 
BOOL 
Value is a Boolean with the usual range of values. 
 Allow values are: “true” and  “false” or  
 “1” and “0” where 1 = true and 0 = false. 
Encourage the use of true & false 
for clarity.  Discourage the use of 1 
& 0. 
XDT 
Value is a date and time in the XML dateTime 
format.  See XML datatype “dateTime”.  The 
dateTime format is:  
 YYYY-MM-DDThh:mm:ss.s+Z 
The fields are as follows: 
 (1) YYYY specifies the year,  
 (2) MM specifies the month, 01 to 12,  
 (3) DD specifies the day of  the month, 01 to 
31, 
 (4) “T” separates the date and time fields, 
 (5) hh specifies the hour, 00 to 23,  
 (6) mm specifies the minute, 00 to 59, 
 (7) ss specifies the second, 00 to 59, and  
 (8) s+ specifies fractional seconds.  
All CPHD date and time parameters 
are specified in UTC (Coordinated 
Universal Time).  The trailing “Z” 
indicates the value is in UTC.  
The decimal point & fractional 
seconds may be omitted.  Any 
number of fractional second digits 
may be included.  
The number of digits included 
DOES NOT indicate the accuracy of 
the date-time value.  Accuracy may 
be specified per Product Design 
Document.

be positive, negative or zero.  See XML datatype

in some products will exceed the 
range supported by a 32-bit binary 
integer format.

10.2 Binary Parameter Formats 
The allowed binary formats are listed in Table 10-2.  For CPHD product files, all binary 
parameters are formatted with the Most Significant Byte (MSB) stored first (i.e., binary data 
is in Big Endian format). 
 
Table 10-2  Allowed Binary Formats  
Binary data formats for Support Arrays and Per Vector Parameters. 
Format Strings Description & Guidance 
Unsigned Integer 
“U1”, “U2”, “U4”, “U8” Unsigned integer (UINT) of size n bytes.  Allow sizes n = 1, 2, 4, and 8 bytes.

“I1”, “I2”, “I4”, “I8” 
Signed integer (INT) of size n bytes.  Allowed sizes n = 1, 2, 4, and 8 bytes.

Floating Point 
“F4”, “F8” 
Floating point format (FLT) of size n bytes.  Allow sizes n = 4 and 8 bytes. 
Floating point formats are IEEE Binary32 or Binary64.

“CI2”, “CI4”, 
“CI8”, “CI16” 
Complex number formed from two signed integer format numbers, each 
signed integer of size n/2 bytes.  Allowed sizes n = 2, 4, 8 and 16.  Real and

Complex number formed from two floating point format numbers, each 
floating point of size n/2 bytes.  Allowed sizes n = 8 and 16.  Real and 
imaginary components in adjacent bytes with real component stored first, 
imaginary component stored second.  CF8 = 8 Bytes, CF16 = 16 Bytes. 
Strings 
“S[1-9][0-9]*” String of size n.  Allowed sizes n > 0.  S5 = 5 Bytes, S30 = 30 Bytes. 
Multiple Parameters 
Used to specify data elements that are formed from 2 or more parameters.  
Individual parameters may be of any allowed format.  Each element is 
described by “<name>=<format_string>;”  The multiple parameter format 
string comprises at least two of these concatenated together. 
For example: “A=U2;B=I2;C=F4;D=CF8;E=S32;”

11  CPHD XML Structure 
The CPHD XML metadata describes how the data in a given product was collected and 
processed.  The XML metadata also specifies the content of the other data blocks in the 
product.  The XML structure is described in this section and is formally defined by the 
CPHD XML schema.  The XML structure contains 15 primary branches.  The structure 
contains 8 primary branches that are required and included in all XML instances.  The 
structure contains 7 optional branches that may be included to provide a more complete 
description of the data in the product.  The primary branches of the CPHD XML are listed 
below and separated into required and optional branches. 
 Required XML Branches Optional XML Branches 
 CPHD / CollectionID CPHD / SupportArray  
 CPHD / Global CPHD / Antenna 
 CPHD / SceneCoordinates CPHD / TxRcv 
 CPHD / Data CPHD / ErrorParameters 
 CPHD / Channel CPHD / ProductInfo 
 CPHD / PVP CPHD / GeoInfo  ** 
 CPHD / Dwell CPHD / MatchInfo 
 CPHD / ReferenceGeometry 
** The CPHD / GeoInfo branch may be repeated in a given instance.  Use of the 
GeoInfo branch is defined per the product design process.  
The CPHD XML structure is graphically illustrated in Figure 11-1.  The XML structure is 
described in the Tables 11-1 through 11-15 below.  Each table describes one of the primary 
branches.  In the tables that follow, the required primary branches and blocks of parameters 
are highlighted in blue.  The optional branches and blocks of parameters are highlighted in 
yellow.  It also includes some components that, if included, allow the choice of the exact 
component to be included.  The components selected by a choice are highlighted in orange. 
The CPHD data structure includes many components and/or elements that may be repeated in 
a given product.  The XML structure includes elements (or blocks of elements) that are 
repeated, as needed, to uniquely describe each of the repeated components.  The repeated 
components are referenced by text-based identifiers.  For example, each data channel is 
referenced by a unique Channel Identifier (denoted Ch_ID).  The parameters that specify the 
signal array and PVP array sizes and locations are repeated for each channel in the CPHD / 
Data branch.  The parameters that specify the signal array content are repeated for each 
channel in the CPHD / Channel branch. 
For a CPHD product that includes a Support block, each support array is referenced by a 
unique Support Array Identifier (SA_ID).  The parameters that specify the support array size 
and location are repeated for each array in the CPHD / Data branch.  The parameters that 
specify the support array content are repeated for each array in the CPHD / SupportArray 
branch.  Other components that are referenced by text-based identifiers are shown in Figure 
11-1.

 PN = E{ Re(Sig)2 + Im(Sig)2 }

 Allowed values:

12  Conformance Guidance 
For a SAR data processing system that creates CPHD products, guidance is provided on 
assessing that the system conforms to the standard.  A method for testing conformance to the 
CPHD standard is also specified. 
12.1 Conformance Description 
For a set of SAR sensors and the Data Processing System that inputs the initial PHD and 
outputs CPHD products, conformance to the standard is assessed as follows.  For a selected 
set of imaging collections, the Data Processing System is tasked to generate one or more 
CPHD data products for each of the collections.  The collections are selected to be a 
representative set of the capabilities of the SAR sensors.  For each collection, the number of 
products to be formed is based on the options that may be selected when forming the CPHD 
products.  Conformance to the CPHD standard is an assessment made about each product 
formed and about the Data Processing System.  For the Data Processing System, the system 
is judged to conform to the standard when all of the CPHD products formed are judged to 
conform to the standard. 
The conformance assessment for the CPHD products formed from a given imaging collection 
is as follows.  Shown in Figure 12-1 is the processing workflow that forms the CPHD 
products.  For the example shown, Sensor X is the SAR sensor that collected the initial PHD.  
For the collection shown, the CPHD product design allows for three possible CPHD products 
to be formed.  The allowed product design options are specified in the Product Design 
Document (PDD).  For a given sensor and processing option, the PDD will also specify the 
conditional and optional metadata parameters to be included.  See Section 3.  For a given 
product, the set of metadata included is referred to as the metadata profile.  For each CPHD 
product, the product is assessed for conformance using the tests defined in the Abstract Test 
Suite (ATS).  A given product shall pass all tests and meet all requirements of the ATS for it 
to claim to conform to the standard.

12.2 Abstract Test Suite 
The Abstract Test Suite (ATS) is a set of test modules and test cases that are applied to a 
CPHD product file.  The ATS is designed to assess that a given product meets a basic level 
of conformance to the CPHD standard.  A primary objective of the ATS is to ensure that the 
CPHD products are correctly formatted and may be ingested by the applications that input 
CPHD products.  Products that meet the requirements of the ATS will be ready to support 
further system testing to ensure the SAR image products that are formed from the CPHD are 
of acceptable quality.   
The ATS consists of three test modules and a total of nine test cases.  The ATS may be 
applied to any CPHD product file.  The ATS test modules are summarized below. 
Test Module 1 Product File Format 
 Set of test cases to verify the product file is correctly formatted. 
Test Module 2 XML Format & Content 
 Set of test cases to verify the XML instance is correctly formatted. 
Test Module 3 Binary Data Block Sizes 
 Set of tests to verify the binary-formatted data block sizes are correct. 
 
12.2.1 Module 1:  Product File Format 
 Purpose: To verify the CPHD product file is correctly formatted. 
 Method: The File Header contains information about the contents of the file.  The 
parameters in the header are used to verify the size and placement of the 
data blocks in the file. 
 Reference:   Section 2; Figure 2-1; Figure 2-2; Table 2-1; 
 Test 1.1 File Header Format 
 Purpose: To verify the File Header is correctly formatted. 
 Method: Examine the File Header to verify that it meets the required format and 
content.  Verify that the required Key-Value Pairs (KVPs) are included.  
Verify that added KVPs specified in the PDD, if any, are included and 
correctly formatted. 
 Reference: Section 2.1; Table 2-1; 
 Test 1.2 Data Block Order & Placement 
 Purpose: To verify the CPHD data blocks are ordered correctly in the file. 
 Method: Examine the KVPs that specify the size and placement of each data block 
in the file.  Verify the required data blocks are included.  Verify that the 
data blocks are ordered correctly.  If fill bytes are included between 
blocks, verify that the fill bytes are as specified. 
 Reference: Section 2; Figure 2-1; Figure 2-2; 
 
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
180 
12.2.2 Module 2:  XML Format & Content 
 Purpose: To verify the XML instance is correctly formatted. 
 Method: The XML instance is examined and compared to the schema to validate 
the instance.  Key XML parameters are examined to verify correct and 
consistent values. 
 Reference:   Section 10; Section 11; CPHD XML schema; Product Description 
Document (PDD); 
 Test 2.1 XML Schema Validation 
 Purpose: To verify the XML instance is correctly formed. 
 Method: Use the CPHD XML schema to validate the XML instance.   
 Reference: Section 10; Section 11; CPHD XML schema; 
 Test 2.2 Collection Information 
 Purpose: To verify the Collection Information parameters are correct. 
 Method: Verify the CPHD / CollectionInfo values are correct for the collection.  
Verify the Classification and ReleaseInfo values in the File Header are 
matched to the values in the XML structure.  Verify CollectorName and 
CoreName are correct for the sensor and the collection. 
 Reference: Table 2-1; Table 11-1; PDD; 
 Test 2.3 Data Channels & Channel Identifiers 
 Purpose: To verify the number of data channels and Channel Identifiers are correct. 
 Method: The XML structure specifies the number of data channels and the text-
based Channel Identifiers (Ch_IDs).  Verify Ch_ID values are consistent 
between the CPHD / Data branch and the CPHD / Channel branch.  Verify 
the Ch_ID values are correct for the type of collection.   
 Reference: Figure 11-1; Table 11-4; Table 11-5; PDD; 
 Test 2.4 XML Metadata Profile 
 Purpose: To verify the XML metadata profile is correct. 
 Method: Verify the XML metadata profile is correct for the collection and the 
processing options selected when forming the CPHD.  Verify all 
conditional parameters are correctly selected.  Verify all optional 
parameters are included per the product design. 
 Reference: Section 11; Tables 11-1 thru 11-15, PDD; 
 
12.2.3 Module 3:  Binary Data Block Sizes 
 Purpose: To verify that the binary-formatted data block sizes are correct and 
consistent with the XML parameter values that describe them. 
 Method: For each binary-formatted data block in the product file, verify that the 
block size and XML parameters that specify the size and shape of the 
block are consistent. 
 Reference:   Table 2-1; Table 11-4; Table 11-5; Table 11-7. 
CPHD Design & Implementation Description Document 
NGA.STND.0068-1_V1.1.0 _CPHD (2021-11-30) 
181 
 Test 3.1 Signal Block Size 
 Purpose: To verify the Signal Block size and the XML parameters that describe it 
are consistent. 
 Method: Compute the signal array size (in bytes) for each data channel in the 
product.  Verify the size of the Signal block in the File Header is equal to 
the sum of the sizes of the signal arrays contained in the product.  Verify 
that the signal array byte offsets are correct. 
 Reference: Section 2.1; Section 2.5; Table 2-1; Table 11-4; Figure 2-7; 
 Test 3.2 PVP Block Size 
 Purpose: To verify the PVP Block size and the XML parameters that describe it are 
consistent. 
 Method: Compute the PVP array size (in bytes) for each data channel in the 
product.  Verify the size of the PVP block in the File Header is equal to 
the sum of the sizes of the PVP arrays contained in the product.  Verify 
that the PVP array byte offsets are correct. 
 Reference: Section 2.1; Section 2.5; Table 2-1; Table 11-4; Figure 2-4; 
 Test 3.3 Support Block Size 
 Purpose: For a product that includes a Support Block, to verify the Support size and 
the XML parameters that describe it are consistent. 
 Method: Compute the Support array size (in bytes) for each support array in the 
product.  Verify the size of the PVP block in the File Header is equal to 
the sum of the sizes of the PVP arrays contained in the product.  Verify 
that the support array byte offsets are correct. 
 Reference: Section 2.1; Section 2-3; Table 2-1; Table 11-4; Figure 2-3;
